          "exact_quote": "There are fixed constants $C$ such that either $h\\leq Cd^C$, or there is one system of type <a href=\"#loc:single\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:single\">[loc:single]</a>, <a href=\"#loc:paired\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:paired\">[loc:paired]</a> or <a href=\"#loc:exception\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:exception\">[loc:exception]</a>, with <a href=\"#loc:base-equations\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:base-equations\">[loc:base-equations]</a>, having the following properties. Its target is an algebraic point $z=(a,U)$ in an affine space of fixed dimension, and it holds at all embeddings in a weighted subcollection $V\\subset V_0$. Writing each scalar expression as $$E(a,U)=\\sum_{j\\geq0}a^j e_j(U),$$ the numerical and function hypotheses of hold: $$\\begin{aligned}\n h_{\\mathrm{aff}}(z)&\\leq Cd^C h,\n &\\sum_{v\\in V}w_v\\log\\frac1{|a|_v}&\\geq\\frac{h}{Cd^C},\n \\label{loc:group-height-mass}\\\\\n \\sum_{v\\in V}w_v\\log^+\\|U\\|_v\n   &\\leq Cd^C(1+\\log h)^C,\n &\\deg e_j&\\leq C(1+j),\n \\label{loc:group-local-degree}\\\\\n h_{\\mathrm{aff}}(e_j)\n   &\\leq C(1+j+d)^C(1+\\log h)^C,\n \\label{loc:coefficient-height}\\\\\n \\left|\\sum_{j\\geq l}a^j e_j(U)\\right|_v\n   &\\leq |a|_v^l\\exp\\bigl((1+l)b_v\\bigr),\\notag\\\\\n \\sum_{v\\in V}w_v b_v&\\leq Cd^C(1+\\log h)^C.\n \\label{loc:tail-bound}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Here $l\\geq0$, $b_v\\geq0$, and one common choice of $b_v$ works for the bounded number of expressions. Each expression is a polynomial combination of fixed bounded degree of the coordinates, fixed regular algebraic germs and fixed ordinary horizontal matrix germs, evaluated at $a$ and, in type <a href=\"#loc:paired\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:paired\">[loc:paired]</a>, at $aR$. Only a fixed finite list of such germs is used. Constants in the expressions may depend on the target, with the bounds displayed above.*\n\n*There are at most $Cd^C$ groups in the construction. This bound counts ordered conjugate tuples, not elements of the Galois group of a normal closure. The proposition asserts no nonidentity condition on an algebraic subvariety through $z$.",
          "source_id": "PM-016.4"
        }

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-fine-cover** [claim](#stmt-geo-setup) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-setup): Take a dominating component of a fixed fine moduli pullback, normalize, omit finite exceptional sets, and choose an interior square center under the infinite-locus supposition. -> A smooth family with fixed covers and controlled lift degrees.
- **step-orthogonal-system** [claim](#stmt-geo-five-space) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-five-space): Solve the self-adjoint trace-free matrix equations, compute the trace form, identify the spin representation, and apply Rosati positivity to actual endomorphisms. -> The split operator system, odd-prime lattices, and positive rational square planes.
- **step-full-joint-monodromy** [claim](#stmt-geo-monodromy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-monodromy): Apply monodromy normality, exclude constant moduli by bounded-domain continuation, and lift an adjoint graph relation to a positive rational homology similitude. -> Full monodromy and the product-or-isogeny joint alternative.
- **step-finite-hecke-types** [claim](#stmt-geo-hecke-list) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-hecke-list): Show analytic curve stabilizers are discrete, prove finite quotient area, deduce finite index, and classify rational transporters into finitely many integral double classes. -> A finite correspondence list and fixed positive multipliers chosen before auxiliary primes.
- **step-finite-field-incidences** [claim](#stmt-inc-finite-fields) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-finite-fields): Use geometric plane positions, symplectic lifting, and bounded-degree rational charts to realize both incidence types over sufficiently large odd finite fields. -> Independent degenerate and nondegenerate plane sums.
- **step-connected-auxiliary-cover** [claim](#stmt-inc-markings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-markings): Apply strong approximation first to geometric monodromy and then to its first-level kernel, obtaining independent product markings on a connected cover. -> Selected lifts with both auxiliary incidences and uniformly bounded relative degrees.
- **step-common-toric-space** [claim](#stmt-inc-torus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-torus): Construct the finite toric torsion part over a common strict henselian regular base and take compatible inverse limits along traits. -> A common marked toric subspace and saturated endomorphism-stable Tate lattice.
- **step-operator-stabilizer** [claim](#stmt-inc-stabilizer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-stabilizer): Separate the symplectic and Lagrangian cases for the preserved subspace and compute the trace pairing on its self-adjoint stabilizer. -> The impossibility of a nondegenerate operator four-space preserving that subspace.
- **step-exclude-bad-center-disks** [claim](#stmt-inc-bad-disks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-inc-bad-disks): Identify potential toric rank at a bad square center, force common specialization on a fixed actual-branch disk, and contradict the nondegenerate auxiliary incidence. -> Fixed bad-center disks containing no selected targets, uniformly over ramification.
- **step-small-endomorphism-generators** [claim](#stmt-ht-endomorphisms) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ht-endomorphisms): Compare ample and Faltings heights, bound the endomorphism definition field, apply generator estimates, bound the full discriminant, and project integral generators to the symmetric trace-free plane. -> Small full-algebra generators and a small labeled plane pair over a bounded relative extension.
- **step-biextension-integral-model** [claim](#stmt-ht-integral-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ht-integral-model): Extend normalized biextensions by a divisorial argument, construct the infinitesimally rigidified torsor, and extend endomorphism actions on traits. -> A locally free homology lattice preserved by integral endomorphisms.
- **step-frame-denominators** [claim](#stmt-ht-frame-costs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ht-frame-costs): Clear fixed rational-frame denominators using line-bundle sections on proper graph models and use fixed center neighborhoods for sharper local costs. -> Global finite frame costs and bounded costs near potentially-good centers.
- **step-plane-coordinate-bounds** [claim](#stmt-ht-coordinates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ht-coordinates): Combine integral finite-place actions with Hodge operator norms and regular-singular frame growth; handle transported pairs by their fixed denominators. -> Global coordinate height bounds and small positive local logarithms before any degree-only height conclusion.
- **step-proximity-mass** [claim](#stmt-loc-mass) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-mass): Use the center divisor height, subtract fixed disk losses, and bound convergence-cutoff losses by prime support and normalized valuation weights. -> Large weighted nearby proximity mass without a bound on the number of proximity primes.
- **step-rational-good-comparison** [claim](#stmt-loc-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-comparison): Compare good lifts with the same proper special fiber by rational crystalline and rigid cohomology and identify the change of evaluations with ordinary horizontal transport. -> Functorial common-reduction trace identities allowing arbitrary ramification.
- **step-spread-center-isogenies** [claim](#stmt-loc-center-isogenies) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-center-isogenies): Place fixed-multiplier isogeny graphs in a finite-type Hilbert scheme and remove the finitely many primes supporting additional special-fiber possibilities. -> A finite list of characteristic-zero center isogenies covering retained specializations.
- **step-common-local-equations** [claim](#stmt-loc-equations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-equations): Use rational traces at fixed places, simultaneous Frobenius conjugation elsewhere, and fixed center isogenies on the exceptional list. -> Single, paired, and exceptional systems with trace, saturation, and transport compatibility.
- **step-group-numerical-inputs** [claim](#stmt-loc-groups) / [proof](#proof-stmt-loc-groups): Count ordered conjugate tuple values, choose a group carrying enough proximity mass, and bound coordinates, coefficients, and tails using fixed ordinary systems. -> The numerical and fixed-function interpolation hypotheses, or an already bounded height.
- **step-continue-branch-identities** [claim](#stmt-nid-continuation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nid-continuation): Use convergent expansions on normalized boundary branches, algebraic density of an analytic open, and continuation of pulled-back ordinary connections. -> Actual curve projections and monodromy continuation of hypothetical identities.
- **step-matrix-span** [claim](#stmt-nid-matrix-span) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nid-matrix-span): Use irreducibility for nonconstant coefficients and signed diagonal and permutation matrices for the full matrix span. -> Tensor equalities forced by dense monodromy pairing identities.
- **step-exclude-frobenius-signs** [claim](#stmt-nid-frobenius) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nid-frobenius): Compose Frobenius with the inverse fixed-multiplier isogeny; exclude the scalar case by valuation parity and the negative case by integral orthogonal eigenspace splitting. -> Neither common sign can identify transported actual center and target pairs.
- **step-universal-nonidentity** [claim](#stmt-nid-nonidentity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nid-nonidentity): Apply full monodromy to single equations, product monodromy away from the closed Hecke list to paired equations, and tensor equalities followed by the sign obstruction to exceptional equations. -> Nonidentity on every required algebraic normalized boundary branch.
- **step-effective-algebra** [claim](#stmt-int-algebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-int-algebra): Combine fixed-variable degree bounds with absolute-height linear algebra, arithmetic intersection bounds, and Chow-form coefficient equations. -> Polynomial degree and coefficient-height control for certificates and components.
- **step-boundary-multiplicity** [claim](#stmt-int-multiplicity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-int-multiplicity): Slice transversely, encode polynomial germ combinations in fixed-rank connections, bound derivative rows by polar degrees and Smith normal form, and avoid cancellation with a bounded integral combination. -> A controlled truncation with poles along every normalized boundary divisor.
- **step-graph-boundary** [claim](#stmt-int-graph) / [proof](#proof-stmt-int-graph): Pass to the finite normalization, use the reciprocal graph function near its boundary poles, and fix the graph coordinate for the incidence bound. -> A projected boundary dimension saving and controlled graph equations.
- **step-layered-interpolation** [claim](#stmt-int-auxiliary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-int-auxiliary): Impose successive linear normal-form conditions, retain exact high parameter powers in membership certificates, and compare the condition count with independent polynomial restrictions. -> An auxiliary polynomial nonzero on the graph in the actual ideal, with freely chosen integral order-to-degree ratio.
- **step-common-parameter-height** [claim](#stmt-int-height) / [proof](#proof-stmt-int-auxiliary-2): Lift to the rational graph, evaluate the certificate only at selected places, evaluate the low-degree polynomial elsewhere, apply the product formula, and descend dimension when its target value vanishes. -> A polynomial bound for the target height.
- **step-selected-lift-height** [claim](#stmt-nid-height) / [proof](#proof-stmt-nid-height): Match all grouped numerical and fixed-function hypotheses and the universal nonidentity condition with the common-parameter height theorem. -> Uniform polynomial heights for selected non-CM square lifts.
- **step-coarse-orbit-bound** [claim](#stmt-fin-orbit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-orbit): Descend through the fixed cover, combine height and full-order discriminant estimates, identify regular and homology traces, and restore finite omissions. -> Uniform coarse-point Galois orbit lower bounds.
- **step-square-locus-coverage** [claim](#stmt-fin-squares) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-squares): Retain the actual polarized lattice in the elliptic tensor decomposition, construct the corresponding Shimura subdatum, and identify its integral commutant with the geometric endomorphism ring. -> Exact square-locus coverage and equality of counting complexity with the full-order discriminant.
- **step-square-finiteness** [claim](#stmt-intro-squares) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-squares-2): Apply the published orbit-to-finiteness implication to the entire square locus after establishing algebraicity, complexity agreement, and the coarse orbit bound. -> Finiteness of the elliptic-square locus without a boundary hypothesis.
- **step-companion-branch-inputs** [claim](#stmt-intro-branches) / [proof](#pm-h-3): Use the separately stated companion finiteness theorems with their full endomorphism-algebra and CM-factor regimes. -> Finiteness of the remaining special-curve branches.
- **step-special-curve-types** [claim](#stmt-fin-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-classification): Compare Hermitian-domain dimensions in the surface Hodge-group classification and apply reducibility to nonsimple surfaces. -> Disjoint coverage of Hodge-generic special-curve points and identification of nongeneric points as special.
- **step-full-zilber-pink** [claim](#stmt-intro-zp) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-classification-2): Apply André–Oort to special points and combine the classified special-curve branches with their separate finiteness theorems. -> Finiteness for all special subvarieties in the stated dimension range.
- **step-special-closure** [claim](#stmt-fin-special-closure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fin-special-closure): Separate cases by the dimension of the smallest special closure and use the full theorem, André–Oort, or the empty locus. -> The special-closure formulation for arbitrary algebraic curves.

In particular, $p\\nmid m_*$ and $p\\ne \\ell_1$. Set $$\\label{nid:psi}\n \\psi=\\phi^{-1}F\\in\\mathop{\\mathrm{End}}(A_0)\\otimes\\mathbb Q,\n \\qquad \\nu(\\psi)=\\frac{p}{m_*},\n \\qquad g=\\mathop{\\mathrm{Ad}}(\\psi)\\big|_{\\mathcal P_{\\mathbb Q_{\\ell_1}}}.$$ Functoriality of specialization preserves <a href=\"#nid:sign-equalities\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"nid:sign-equalities\">[nid:sign-equalities]</a>. Moreover $F$ conjugates each unprimed labeled endomorphism to its primed specialization, for both the center and target labels using this same $F$. Composing with $\\phi^{-1}$ therefore shows that $g$ acts by $\\epsilon$ on each of the two specialized rational endomorphism planes.",
          "source_id": "PM-016.4"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "nid:lattice-splitting",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose instead that $\\epsilon=-1$. Then $g^2=1$ and its minus eigenspace is exactly $H_{\\ell_1}$. Consequently $\\psi$, its inverse, and their conjugation action preserve $\\Lambda$. The trace pairing on $\\Lambda$ is unimodular, as in [geo:five-space](#geo:five-space) and the choice of $\\ell_1$ in [inc:markings](#inc:markings). Since $\\ell_1$ is odd, the integral idempotents $$e_+=\\frac{1+g}{2},\\qquad e_-=\\frac{1-g}{2}$$ give an orthogonal direct sum $$\\label{nid:lattice-splitting}\n \\Lambda=\\Lambda_+\\mathbin{\\perp}\\Lambda_-,\n \\qquad \\Lambda_\\pm=e_\\pm\\Lambda,\n \\qquad \\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}\\Lambda_-=4.$$ Orthogonality follows from $\\langle v_+,v_-\\rangle=\\langle gv_+,gv_-\\rangle\n=-\\langle v_+,v_-\\rangle$. In bases of these two direct summands the Gram determinant of $\\Lambda$ is the product of their integral Gram determinants. It is a unit, so both determinants are units. Smith normal form over $\\mathcal O$, after clearing the entry poles, has nonzero exponents $\\sigma_1\\leq\\cdots\\leq\\sigma_{s'}$ satisfying $$\\sigma_i\\geq-P,\\qquad\n \\sum_{i=1}^{s'}\\sigma_i\n   =\\min_{\\Delta'\\ne0}\\mathop{\\mathrm{ord}}_0\\Delta'\n   \\leq s'P.$$ Consequently $\\sigma_{s'}\\leq(2s'-1)P$. The invertible row and column matrices in Smith normal form preserve the local lattices and their maximal ideals. The saturated kernel corresponds to the zero columns, so primitivity modulo $K$ means that some coordinate in a nonzero column is a unit. It follows that $$\\label{int:smith-order}\n \\min_{0\\leq j<s'}\\mathop{\\mathrm{ord}}_0(\\delta^j f)\n   \\leq (2s'-1)P.$$",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-elliptic-square-finiteness**: The non-CM elliptic-square locus on the stated Hodge-generic curve is finite, allowing varying elliptic curves, isogenies and principal polarizations.
- **inv-selected-lift-height**: The shifted ample height of every selected non-CM elliptic-square lift has a polynomial bound in its field degree, with constants depending only on the fixed data.
- **inv-universal-branch-nonidentity**: Every selected equation group has a nonzero analytic germ on every stated normalized boundary branch of every algebraic subvariety through its target. The operator coordinates on these subvarieties need not represent Hodge endomorphisms.
- **inv-projected-graph-boundary**: Under the strict pole hypothesis on every normalized boundary divisor, the projected graph boundary and the boundary incidence with the graph coordinate fixed have dimension at most $k-2$. When $k=1$ these sets are empty.
- **inv-actual-ideal-membership**: Layered interpolation produces a polynomial nonzero on the graph variety with membership in the actual ideal, retaining the prescribed power of the parameter without taking a radical.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-effective-enumeration**: Turn finiteness or the polynomial height estimate into an explicit terminating enumeration of all elliptic-square points. -> The source does not supply numerical constants or a point enumeration procedure; an existence bound alone does not provide an executable stopping criterion.
- **anti-nongeneric-full-locus**: Apply the full special-subvariety finiteness conclusion to an arbitrary curve without checking Hodge genericity. -> The full theorem assumes Hodge genericity; the result for an arbitrary curve instead restricts the dimensions of special subvarieties relative to its smallest special closure.
- **anti-global-height-only**: Apply interpolation using only a global bound on the target coordinates. -> The ideal-membership certificate can have greater degree than the auxiliary polynomial, so its evaluation requires the much smaller positive coordinate logarithms at the selected places.
- **anti-boundary-value-test**: Verify nonidentity only by evaluating the equations on a boundary divisor, or only on subvarieties parametrizing actual endomorphisms. -> The descent chooses new algebraic subvarieties using auxiliary coefficients; the required condition concerns whole analytic branches of arbitrary such subvarieties.
- **anti-unsaturated-pair-reduction**: Replace the reduction of a saturated endomorphism plane with the span of the chosen integral pair reduced modulo the auxiliary prime. -> A rationally spanning pair can become dependent modulo that prime and need not be a basis of the plane lattice.
- **anti-whole-graph-boundary**: Treat the projected-boundary dimension estimate as a bound on the whole graph boundary. -> Fibers of the graph boundary projection may have positive dimension; the incidence bound uses an additional equation fixing the graph coordinate.
- **anti-isolated-hecke-degree-bound**: Use the finite correspondence list to bound the isogeny degrees of every isolated pair of points on the fixed curves. -> The finite list controls dominating relations; it does not impose such a bound on isolated pairs outside those correspondences.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results establish finiteness and the existence of height and orbit bounds for algebraic points, rather than a finite executable construction enumerating those points. The source states: "The constants may depend on $C$; effectivity is not required." The interpolation application also requires nonidentity on every normalized boundary branch of every algebraic subvariety through the target, as stated in the conditional height theorem and the nonidentity proposition. Finite checks of the linear algebra or ideal-membership ingredients would not decide these universal hypotheses or certify the finiteness theorem.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finiteness of the elliptic-square locus does not supply an executable point enumerator, an explicit stopping threshold, or a software interface. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Horizontal transport compares homological realizations of abelian varieties; it does not define a simulation state, an evolution rule for gameplay, or a physical update model. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The orbit-to-finiteness implication supplies a mathematical criterion whose constants may depend on the curve. It provides no explicit search budget or ready-to-use computational integration. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Layered interpolation gives a finite construction: fix linear normal-form reductions, impose successive linear conditions on polynomial coefficients, and solve a bounded-degree certificate system to obtain a polynomial nonzero on the variety and belonging to the actual ideal. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-hodge-generic-curve-regime** (hypothesis): The elliptic-square finiteness theorem requires a nonempty reduced irreducible closed algebraic curve over the algebraic closure of the rationals that is Hodge generic in the stated moduli variety. Blocked use: ['Apply the main finiteness theorem to a curve contained in a proper special subvariety.', 'Extend the conclusion to higher-dimensional subvarieties or different ambient moduli spaces.']
- **limit-special-closure-threshold** (formal_scope): For an arbitrary curve, the special-closure corollary imposes its own dimension restriction relative to the smallest containing special subvariety. Blocked use: ['Replace the special-closure dimension restriction with the unrestricted special-curve locus of the Hodge-generic theorem.']
- **limit-no-effective-enumeration-contract** (nonconstructive): The finiteness argument does not require effective constants and supplies no explicit point-enumeration stopping bound. Blocked use: ['Certify completion of a point search from finiteness alone.', 'Treat the existential height bound as a supplied numerical search threshold.']
- **limit-fixed-cover-and-center-selection** (hypothesis): The interior center is chosen under the supposition of an infinite elliptic-square locus. Covers, omissions and auxiliary data remain fixed throughout the varying-target argument. Blocked use: ['Choose new covers or centers independently for every target while retaining the same asserted uniform constants.', 'Infer an effective center-selection procedure for an arbitrary input curve.']
- **limit-dominating-hecke-relations** (formal_scope): The finite correspondence list controls dominating relations between the fixed coarse curves; it does not bound isogeny degrees for isolated pairs. Blocked use: ['Assign a fixed multiplier to an isolated pair without proving membership in a retained correspondence.']
- **limit-finite-field-incidence-regime** (hypothesis): The plane-position lemma requires an odd finite field of sufficiently large cardinality and a nondegenerate center plane. The target plane may be degenerate. Blocked use: ['Apply the incidence existence claim in characteristic two.', 'Ignore the field-size threshold or center-plane nondegeneracy.', 'Require target-plane nondegeneracy as an additional source hypothesis.']
- **limit-saturated-plane-reductions** (hypothesis): Auxiliary incidence conditions concern separately saturated plane lattices. A rationally spanning integral pair need not give the same plane after reduction. Blocked use: ['Test an auxiliary incidence using only the reductions of the chosen small endomorphisms without establishing that they form a lattice basis.']
- **limit-independent-auxiliary-level-roles** (hypothesis): The auxiliary markings impose different incidences and different prime exclusions. Their roles in the sign obstruction and bad-center disk exclusion cannot be interchanged without a new proof. Blocked use: ['Use the degenerate incidence to exclude a common toric subspace.', 'Omit the exclusions involving fixed Hecke multipliers or bad-center residue characteristics.']
- **limit-common-toric-local-base** (hypothesis): The common marked toric subspace is established for traits through the same geometric toric point on the specified regular model, with the marking prime invertible. Blocked use: ['Identify toric torsion labels from unrelated specializations.', 'Apply the common-subspace assertion when the marking prime equals the residue characteristic.']
- **limit-bad-center-comparison-exclusion** (formal_scope): Selected targets are excluded from sufficiently small actual-center disks at non-potentially-good center places. The retained comparison argument uses common good reduction. Blocked use: ['Perform the stated horizontal cohomological comparison at a bad center without first establishing a different comparison theorem.']
- **limit-regular-biextension-base** (hypothesis): The integral Lie-bundle construction assumes the specified regular noetherian integral base, smooth separated semiabelian family with geometrically integral fibers, and principally polarized abelian dense open. Blocked use: ['Apply the biextension extension assertion directly on an arbitrary singular base.', 'Assume the construction asserts integral de Rham duality on a toric special fiber.']
- **limit-distinct-global-and-local-coordinate-controls** (hypothesis): The global coordinate height bound and the sharper selected-place positive logarithm bound are separate inputs. The near-center estimate requires sufficiently close actual branches with bad-center places excluded. Blocked use: ['Replace small positive local coordinate logarithms by the global height estimate.', 'Derive the local estimate only after assuming the degree-only height conclusion.']
- **limit-actual-center-branch-and-weights** (hypothesis): Local proximity uses the actual branch through the fixed center and normalized absolute-height weights. Small parameter values on other branches do not constitute the required center proximity. Blocked use: ['Count every zero of the rational parameter as proximity to the center divisor.', 'Use unnormalized place counts that change under ramified field extension.']
- **limit-rational-versus-integral-comparison** (formal_scope): The comparison between good lifts is rational and allows arbitrary ramification. Preservation of integral lattices is a separate assertion proved using integral models. Blocked use: ['Infer integral crystalline lattice preservation from the rational comparison.', 'Interpret arbitrary ramification as permission to discard the common good-reduction hypothesis.']
- **limit-fixed-multiplier-specialization** (hypothesis): Special-fiber isogenies lift to the center-isogeny list only for the fixed centers and fixed multipliers, after removing a fixed finite set of residue characteristics. Blocked use: ['Assert that every special-fiber isogeny lifts in characteristic zero.', 'Allow the multiplier to vary while retaining the same specialization exclusions.']
- **limit-common-equations-and-group-count** (hypothesis): Selected groups use common equations and fixed centered germs. The polynomial group bound counts ordered conjugate tuples rather than automorphisms of a normal closure, and the grouping proposition establishes no branch nonidentity. Blocked use: ['Group by every normal-closure automorphism and claim the same degree dependence.', 'Apply the height theorem solely from the grouped numerical estimates.', 'Omit the base-coordinate equations that retain the curve branches.']
- **limit-universal-analytic-branch-nonidentity** (hypothesis): The conditional height theorem requires nonidentity on whole normalized analytic boundary branches of every relevant irreducible algebraic subvariety through the target, including varieties whose operator coordinates are not Hodge classes. Blocked use: ['Check only values on the boundary divisor.', 'Check only the original ambient variety.', 'Restrict the test to subvarieties parametrizing actual endomorphisms.', 'Replace the universal hypothesis by agreement on finitely many numerical samples.']
- **limit-frobenius-sign-arithmetic-data** (hypothesis): The sign obstruction uses actual rational endomorphisms at the target, the common Frobenius reduction, fixed-multiplier isogenies, and the integral prime-to-characteristic saturated-plane incidence. Blocked use: ['Apply the arithmetic sign obstruction directly to unrestricted operator coordinates away from the target.', 'Drop the required coprimality or integral lattice conditions.']
- **limit-full-order-discriminant** (formal_scope): The orbit estimate uses the discriminant of the full geometric endomorphism order with regular-representation trace, including its conductor. Blocked use: ['Substitute the Gram determinant of the symmetric-plane pair.', 'Replace the full geometric order by a smaller generated lattice without accounting for the index.', 'Ignore the discriminant trace convention.']
- **limit-companion-inputs-for-full-theorem** (formal_scope): The full special-subvariety finiteness theorem uses the companion product-with-CM and quaternion-division results together with special-point finiteness. The elliptic-square theorem is independent of those companion inputs. Blocked use: ['Claim the elliptic-square argument alone proves the full theorem.', 'Replace equality of the full quaternion division endomorphism algebra by an embedding into a larger algebra.']
- **limit-fixed-variable-algebraic-bounds** (hypothesis): The polynomial degree and coefficient-height bounds in effective algebra fix the number of variables and use absolute logarithmic heights. They are not stated running-time or memory bounds. Blocked use: ['Let the number of variables grow while retaining the same polynomial estimates.', 'Publish the mathematical degree bounds as computational complexity guarantees.']
- **limit-multiplicity-and-strict-graph-poles** (hypothesis): The graph boundary estimate requires a strict pole on every normalized boundary divisor. The multiplicity lemma supplies this truncation only under the fixed ordinary-function data and branch nonidentity assumptions. Blocked use: ['Apply the graph lemma from nonvanishing on a boundary divisor alone.', 'Ignore a normalized boundary branch when checking the strict order inequality.']
- **limit-projected-boundary-not-whole-boundary** (formal_scope): The graph lemma bounds the projected boundary and the incidence obtained by fixing the graph coordinate. It does not bound the dimension of the entire graph boundary. Blocked use: ['Transfer the dimension saving to the whole graph boundary.', 'Remove the equation fixing the graph coordinate from the incidence used for interpolation.']
- **limit-actual-ideal-and-condition-count** (hypothesis): Layered interpolation requires the stated incidence dimension saving and strict condition-count inequality. Its certificate belongs to the actual ideal; radical membership is insufficient for retaining the prescribed parameter power. Blocked use: ['Replace the ideal by its radical.', 'Claim existence of a nonzero-on-variety auxiliary polynomial without checking the condition count.', 'Treat the unit-ideal curve case as an ordinary nonempty boundary incidence.']
- **limit-product-formula-descent-evaluation** (hypothesis): The descent evaluates the membership certificate at selected places using small positive local coordinate logarithms, and evaluates the lower-degree auxiliary polynomial elsewhere. Universal branch nonidentity must remain available after each strict dimension drop. Blocked use: ['Charge the certificate degree against the global height at every place and claim the same height inequality.', 'Assume nonidentity survives descent without the universal hypothesis.', 'Discard the empty-boundary case from the termination argument.']

Each expression is a polynomial combination of fixed bounded degree of the coordinates, fixed regular algebraic germs and fixed ordinary horizontal matrix germs, evaluated at $a$ and, in type <a href=\"#loc:paired\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"loc:paired\">[loc:paired]</a>, at $aR$. <a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every Hodge-generic algebraic curve in $`\mathcal A_2`$ over $`\overline{\mathbb Q}`$ contains only finitely many points whose abelian surface is isogenous to the square of an elliptic curve without complex multiplication (CM). Combining this result with the companion $`E\times\mathrm{CM}`$ and quaternionic-division finiteness theorems, we prove the curve case of Zilber–Pink in $`\mathcal A_2`$ over $`\overline{\mathbb Q}`$, without a boundary hypothesis.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="intro:section"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\mathcal A_2$ be the coarse moduli variety of principally polarized abelian surfaces. A curve in $\mathcal A_2$ is *Hodge generic* if it is contained in no proper Shimura-special subvariety. The curve case of the Zilber–Pink conjecture predicts that such a curve meets the union of all special subvarieties of dimension at most one in only finitely many points. The word “all” includes every Hecke translate; neither an isogeny degree nor an endomorphism order is fixed.

We first prove the elliptic-square part of this prediction, then combine it with the two companion branch theorems to obtain the full conclusion. For a point $s\in\mathcal A_2(\overline{\mathbb Q})$, write $(A_s,\lambda_s)$ for its principally polarized abelian surface, and set $$\Sigma_{E^2}(C)=
 \bigl\{s\in C(\overline{\mathbb Q}): A_s\sim E^2
       \text{ for an elliptic curve }E/\overline{\mathbb Q}\text{ without CM}\bigr\}.$$ Here $\sim$ denotes isogeny over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$, without any requirement that it respect the principal polarization. The condition is equivalent to $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}}(A_s)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q\simeq M_2(\mathbb Q)$.

<a id="stmt-intro-squares"></a>
<div id="intro:squares" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (The elliptic-square case). *Let $C\subset\mathcal A_2$ be a nonempty reduced irreducible closed algebraic curve over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$. If $C$ is Hodge generic, then $\Sigma_{E^2}(C)$ is finite.*

</div>

No condition is imposed on the closure of $C$ at the boundary of a compactification. In particular, the theorem includes complete curves. The elliptic curve, its isogeny degree and the principal polarization may all vary.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The companion branches and the full theorem

The two remaining special-curve loci are treated by the following companion theorems. We state their exact conclusions to specify the inputs to the full finiteness theorem.

<a id="stmt-intro-branches"></a>
<div id="intro:branches" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2** (The two companion branches). *For every nonempty reduced irreducible closed Hodge-generic curve $C\subset\mathcal A_2$ over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$, the following two subsets of $C(\overline{\mathbb Q})$ are finite:*

1.  *the points for which $A_s$ is isogenous over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ to $E_1\times E_2$, with at least one elliptic factor having CM;*

2.  *the points for which the full algebra $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}}(A_s)\otimes_{\mathbb Z}\mathbb Q$ is an indefinite quaternion division algebra over $\mathbb Q$.*

*These statements allow all CM fields, endomorphism orders, quaternion algebras, isogeny degrees and principal polarizations. They impose no boundary, reduction, integrality, height or field-degree condition.*

</div>

In (B), indefiniteness means that the algebra becomes $M_2(\mathbb R)$ over $\mathbb R$; the division condition excludes $M_2(\mathbb Q)$. An embedding of a quaternion algebra into a larger endomorphism algebra does not satisfy (B). In (A), both-CM products are included. The complete proofs of these conclusions are given respectively in the companion articles (OpenAI 2026b, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[CompanionECM](#ref-CompanionECM)] and (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CompanionQuaternion](#ref-CompanionQuaternion)]. The proof below keeps their use separate from the elliptic-square theorem.

<a id="stmt-intro-zp"></a>
<div id="intro:zp" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Zilber–Pink for curves in $\mathcal A_2$). *For every nonempty reduced irreducible closed Hodge-generic curve $C\subset\mathcal A_2$ over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$, the set $$\Sigma(C)=C(\overline{\mathbb Q})\cap
 \bigcup_{\substack{Z\subset\mathcal A_2\ \mathrm{special}\mathstrut\\
                     \dim Z\le 1}} Z(\overline{\mathbb Q})$$ is finite.*

</div>

resolves positively the Hodge-generic-curve case of the Zilber–Pink conjecture in $\mathcal A_2$ over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$. Its proof combines [intro:squares](#intro:squares), [intro:branches](#intro:branches) with the classification of special curves and André–Oort for special points, as explained in [fin:classification](#fin:classification), [fin:complete](#fin:complete). Theorem <a href="#intro:squares" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:squares">1</a> and its proof are independent of the two companion results. After removing special points, the three branch statements cover the points of $C$ that are Hodge generic on a special curve; [fin:classification](#fin:classification) proves this classification with the full endomorphism algebras specified. For a curve that is not Hodge generic in $\mathcal A_2$, André–Oort also gives the corresponding special-closure formulation: if $S_C$ is the smallest special subvariety containing $C$, then $C$ meets the union of special $Z$ with $\dim Z<\dim S_C-1$ in finitely many points ().

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### History and the arithmetic step

Zilber’s atypical-intersection principle for semiabelian varieties (Zilber 2002) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Zilber2002](#ref-Zilber2002)] and Pink’s formulation for mixed Shimura varieties place this problem in a common unlikely-intersection framework. Pink’s formulation predicts finiteness for a Hodge-generic curve meeting special subvarieties of codimension at least two (Pink 2005, Conjecture 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Pink2005](#ref-Pink2005)]. In the three-dimensional space $\mathcal A_2$, the special curves have three generic types: elliptic products with a CM factor, non-CM elliptic squares, and quaternion-division multiplication. Pila and Tsimerman established André–Oort for $\mathcal A_2$, which supplies the special-point case (Pila and Tsimerman 2013, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[PilaTsimerman2013](#ref-PilaTsimerman2013)]. Daw and Orr developed the corresponding unlikely-intersection arguments for these curve loci (Daw and Orr 2021, 2022) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DawOrrECM2021](#ref-DawOrrECM2021), [DawOrr2022](#ref-DawOrr2022)].

The arithmetic-to-geometric strategy compares lower bounds coming from Galois conjugates with upper bounds for rational points in definable sets. Pila and Zannier introduced this strategy in their proof of Manin–Mumford (Pila and Zannier 2008) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[PilaZannier2008](#ref-PilaZannier2008)], using the counting theorem of Pila and Wilkie (Pila and Wilkie 2006) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[PilaWilkie2006](#ref-PilaWilkie2006)]. In Siegel moduli, the arithmetic difficulty is to obtain a sufficiently large orbit uniformly as the special subvariety varies. Daw and Orr’s quantitative reduction theory supplies the geometric and counting implication for the elliptic-square and quaternionic loci. Their unconditional quaternionic theorem assumes that the curve meets the zero-dimensional Baily–Borel boundary (Daw and Orr 2022, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[DawOrr2022](#ref-DawOrr2022)]; the orbit-to-finiteness implication we use has no such hypothesis.

Daw and Orr subsequently proved the full special-curve finiteness conclusion, including elliptic squares, under the same total multiplicative-degeneration hypothesis (Daw and Orr 2025a, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[DawOrrMultiplicative2025](#ref-DawOrrMultiplicative2025)]. Papas studied splitting points using periods centered at an interior $E\times\mathrm{CM}$ or elliptic-square point. For an everywhere potentially-good center, his height bound depends polynomially on the point degree and the number of supersingular places of proximity; his finiteness corollary fixes an upper bound for that number (Papas 2025, Theorem 1.5 and Corollary 1.6) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[PapasGFunctionsI2025](#ref-PapasGFunctionsI2025)]. His analogous bad-reduction statements are conditional on comparison conjectures (Papas 2025, Theorem 1.8 and Corollary 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[PapasGFunctionsI2025](#ref-PapasGFunctionsI2025)]. In the product $Y(1)^3$ of three modular curves, Daw, Orr and Papas obtain finiteness from an interior basepoint, allowing bad reduction but restricting the growth of the number of supersingular places of proximity (Daw, Orr, and Papas 2026, Theorems 1.6 and 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[DawOrrPapas2026](#ref-DawOrrPapas2026)]. The present height estimate permits bad reduction of the center and does not bound that number of places.

For the elliptic-square locus, the implication we use is Daw–Orr’s (Daw and Orr 2022, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DawOrr2022](#ref-DawOrr2022)]: a suitable polynomial lower bound for Galois orbits in terms of the endomorphism-order discriminant implies finiteness. Their arithmetic hypothesis is stated in (Daw and Orr 2022, Conjecture 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[DawOrr2022](#ref-DawOrr2022)]. We prove the required bound for the entire elliptic-square locus before applying that theorem. The constants may depend on $C$; effectivity is not required.

The main arithmetic result is a polynomial height bound for selected lifts $t$ to one fixed fine-level curve. Write $d=\max\{2,[K(t):K]\}$, where $K$ contains the fixed data, and let $h(t)\geq2$ be a shifted ample logarithmic height. If $s$ is the coarse image of $t$, let $\Delta_s$ be the discriminant of the full geometric order $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\overline{\mathbb Q}}(A_s)$, using the trace of its regular representation. The two arithmetic estimates are $$h(t)\leq c d^{\kappa},\qquad
 \Delta_s\leq c\bigl(dh(t)\bigr)^{\kappa'}.$$ We prove the first in [nid:height](#nid:height); the second comes from the endomorphism estimates of Masser and Wüstholz (Masser and Wüstholz 1994) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MasserWustholz1994](#ref-MasserWustholz1994)], as explained in [ht:endomorphisms](#ht:endomorphisms). Since the cover degree is fixed, $d\leq c[K(s):K]$. Combining the two estimates therefore gives the required orbit bound $[K(s):K]\geq c'\Delta_s^{\delta}$ for some $\delta>0$. The discriminant belongs to the full order, not just the symmetric plane used in our period equations. This is why we bound endomorphisms spanning the whole algebra as well as a pair spanning that plane. [fin:orbit](#fin:orbit), [fin:squares](#fin:squares) account for every fixed omission and identify $\Delta_s$ with the complexity in the counting theorem.

Several classical inputs make this reduction quantitative. André’s normality theorem for connected algebraic monodromy identifies the monodromy group of our Hodge-generic family (André 1992, sec. 5, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Andre1992](#ref-Andre1992)]. Strong approximation then permits independent markings at two auxiliary levels (Matthews, Vaserstein, and Weisfeiler 1984) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MVW1984](#ref-MVW1984)]. Deligne’s regularity theorem and Schmid’s Hodge-norm estimates control fixed algebraic frames near the punctures (Deligne 1970; Schmid 1973) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Deligne1970](#ref-Deligne1970), [Schmid1973](#ref-Schmid1973)], while rational crystalline comparison identifies horizontal transport at nearby good reductions (Berthelot and Ogus 1983; Berthelot 1981--1982) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[BerthelotOgus1983](#ref-BerthelotOgus1983), [Berthelot1982](#ref-Berthelot1982)]. The interpolation argument uses effective polynomial ideal membership and arithmetic intersection bounds (Hermann 1926; Dubé 1990; Aschenbrenner 2004; Krick, Pardo, and Sombra 2001) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Hermann1926](#ref-Hermann1926), [Dube1990](#ref-Dube1990), [Aschenbrenner2004](#ref-Aschenbrenner2004), [KrickPardoSombra2001](#ref-KrickPardoSombra2001)]; its graph construction is what turns those bounds into the height estimate needed here.

The use of arithmetic auxiliary functions and period equations belongs to the tradition of Bombieri’s and André’s work on $G$-functions (Bombieri 1981; André 1989) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Bombieri1981](#ref-Bombieri1981), [Andre1989](#ref-Andre1989)]. For curves in products of modular curves with multiplicative degeneration, Daw and Orr use relations valid simultaneously at all nonarchimedean places, obtained from Tate uniformization (Daw and Orr 2025b, sec. 1.B) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DawOrrModular2025](#ref-DawOrrModular2025)]. Interior $p$-adic periods are developed in André’s motivic account (André 1995) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Andre1995](#ref-Andre1995)]; see also his recent discussion (André 2025, sec. 2.2 and 3.3.4) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[AndreSurvey2025](#ref-AndreSurvey2025)] and (Papas 2025, Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[PapasGFunctionsI2025](#ref-PapasGFunctionsI2025)]. Our interpolation theorem is formulated for fixed differential systems at an ordinary point. [int:section](#int:section) states the theorem and explains its mechanism; [int:proof](#int:proof) proves the varying-curve multiplicity estimate and two-argument graph descent.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The proof mechanism

Suppose the square locus is infinite, and fix one of its points $t_0$ on a smooth fine-level cover. This interior point will be the center of our local equations. On first homology, put $$\mathcal P=\{D:D^\dagger=D,\ \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D)=0\},\qquad
 \langle D,E\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(DE),$$ where $\dagger$ is the adjoint for the polarization. This is a rank-five quadratic system. At a non-CM elliptic square, its actual rational endomorphisms form a positive plane. Choose labeled integral pairs $\mathbf B=(B_1,B_2)$ and $\mathbf P=(P_1,P_2)$ spanning the center and target planes, with the target pair chosen using the endomorphism estimate. Let $a=x(t)$ for a local parameter $x$ vanishing at $t_0$, and let $Y(a)$ be horizontal back-transport in a fixed de Rham frame from the target to the center, with $Y(0)=1$. We study the four cross pairings $$\langle\mathbf B,Y(a)\mathbf P\rangle
   :=\bigl(\langle B_i,Y(a)P_j\rangle\bigr)_{1\leq i,j\leq2}.$$

When the two fibers have the same good reduction, rational cohomological comparison identifies these entries with traces of products of actual endomorphisms of that reduction. Frobenius preserves the traces after simultaneously conjugating both fibers and both pairs. Writing primes for those conjugate data gives the equation $$\langle\mathbf B,Y(a)\mathbf P\rangle
   =\langle\mathbf B',Y'(aR)\mathbf P'\rangle,
 \qquad R=a'/a,\quad |R|_v=1.$$ The residue characteristic no longer occurs in this equation. Grouping places by the two labeled conjugate tuples gives only polynomially many systems in $d$, even when the number of proximity primes is unbounded. At the archimedean and finitely many exceptional places we instead use a single equation $\langle\mathbf B,Y(a)\mathbf P\rangle=\mathbf r$ with a rational matrix $\mathbf r$.

Two fixed prime levels make these equations usable. At the first, $\ell_1$, the separately saturated center and target plane lattices are marked so that their reductions span a degenerate four-space; at the second, $\ell_2$, their sum is nondegenerate. Both conditions can be imposed on one connected finite cover. The second incidence excludes sufficiently close targets at a place where the center is not potentially good: both planes would preserve the same toric two-space, forcing their sum to be degenerate. Thus every retained finite place admits the good-reduction comparison just described.

The first incidence serves a different purpose. Full monodromy rules out identities among the equations unless the target and its conjugate lie on one of a fixed finite list of polarized Hecke correspondences. On that list, isogenies of fixed multiplier transport the primed pairs back to the unprimed fibers. Monodromy then forces any identity to identify the transported pairs with the original pairs by a scalar on the center pair and its reciprocal on the target pair. The center norms force that scalar to be $+1$ or $-1$. The positive sign would make a rational square equal to $p/m$, where $p$ is the residue characteristic and $m$ is a fixed isogeny multiplier prime to $p$. The negative sign contradicts the degenerate incidence at $\ell_1$. The Hecke list and its multipliers are fixed before choosing these levels, so $\ell_1$ can avoid all those multipliers.

There are two different size estimates in the interpolation step. Let $U$ collect the base coordinates, the target endomorphism coordinates and, when needed, $R$. Their global height can grow like $h=h(t)$: $h_{\rm aff}(a,U)\leq Cd^C h$. Unless $h$ is already polynomially bounded in $d$, we select a group of nearby places at which the weighted positive logarithms of $U$ are at most $Cd^C(1+\log h)^C$, while the weighted sum of $\log|a|_v^{-1}$ is at least $h/(Cd^C)$. Here the weights are the normalized absolute-height weights. The center divisor supplies the large proximity sum; integral lattices preserved by endomorphisms and bounded center frames supply the much smaller coordinate sum. These estimates hold before any bound for $h$ in terms of $d$ is known.

turns this imbalance, together with the nonidentity just explained, into $h\leq Cd^C$. Its proof constructs a polynomial whose degree is small compared with its order of local smallness. The product formula either bounds $h$ or forces the target into a proper algebraic subvariety. The process repeats in that subvariety, which explains why nonidentity must hold on boundary branches of arbitrary algebraic varieties through the target, even when their operator coordinates are not Hodge classes. [int:section](#int:section) explains how a graph construction makes this dimension descent quantitative.

The quaternionic companion develops Frobenius cross-pairing interpolation and the degenerate auxiliary incidence (OpenAI 2026a, secs. 2–5) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[CompanionQuaternion](#ref-CompanionQuaternion)]. The arguments needed here, including the second incidence, the integral coordinate bounds and the nonidentity analysis for split endomorphism algebras, are proved below. The interpolation theorem and the separation of bad-center disks from good-reduction comparisons are the parts of the construction that may be useful in other height problems.

Figure <a href="#fig:two-incidences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:two-incidences">1</a> distinguishes the two uses of the auxiliary levels and their roles alongside the arithmetic estimates.

<figure id="fig:two-incidences">

<figcaption>The two level incidences have different roles. The first is used to exclude the negative sign in the nonidentity argument; the positive sign is excluded by the Frobenius multiplier. The second excludes proximity to centers with non-potentially-good reduction. The height conclusion uses common equations at the retained places, nonidentity on algebraic boundary branches, the large proximity sum, and the small positive coordinate logarithms described in the text. The diagram is schematic: all plane positions are in the separately marked finite-field quadratic spaces.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions and organization

All heights are absolute logarithmic heights. Place sums use normalized absolute-height weights, so extending a field preserves the sum for pulled-back quantities. Constants may depend on the curve, the center, fixed levels, frames and other fixed data. They never depend on the varying target, its height or degree, or a varying residue characteristic. In an estimate described as polynomial, the exponent is fixed.

We use covariant first homology and its de Rham and prime-to-residue- characteristic étale realizations. The term *ordinary point* for a differential equation means a point where the connection has no singularity; it places no ordinarity condition on the reduction of an abelian variety. Every reduction of an endomorphism plane uses its saturation in the specified integral lattice.

states the interpolation theorem and explains its proof mechanism. [geo:section](#geo:section) constructs the geometric systems and controls the exceptional Hecke correspondences. [inc:section](#inc:section) gives the two incidences and excludes bad-center disks. [ht:section](#ht:section) proves the coordinate estimates, and [loc:section](#loc:section) constructs the local equations with their quantitative bounds. [nid:section](#nid:section) rules out identities and obtains the height bound. Finally, [fin:section](#fin:section) proves [intro:squares](#intro:squares), [intro:zp](#intro:zp) and the special-closure corollary. The complete proof of the interpolation theorem is given in [int:proof](#int:proof).

<a id="int:section"></a>
### Interpolation with a common small parameter

The height theorem below applies to equations assembled from a fixed collection of ordinary differential systems. Its hypotheses distinguish the global height of the target from the much smaller positive coordinate logarithms at the places used for interpolation. We state the theorem and explain the graph construction that exploits this distinction here. The full proof is given in Appendix <a href="#int:proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:proof">9</a>.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The quantitative statement

For a tuple $x=(x_1,\ldots,x_r)$ over a number field $F$, write $$h_{\mathrm{aff}}(x)
   =\sum_v w_v\log\max(1,|x_1|_v,\ldots,|x_r|_v),
 \qquad w_v=\frac{[F_v:\mathbb Q_v]}{[F:\mathbb Q]}.$$ At an archimedean place we use the ordinary absolute value, so that a complex place has weight $2/[F:\mathbb Q]$. At a finite place above $p$, the absolute value extends $|p|_p=p^{-1}$. The same notation for a polynomial means the height of its coefficient tuple. A weighted subcollection of places may split a place into its embeddings, with the corresponding weights. When $F$ is enlarged, these weights are split among the extensions of each embedding. In particular all sums for quantities defined over the old field are unchanged. We write $\sum_V w_v$ with this convention even when the subcollection is described in terms of embeddings.

An *ordinary germ* below is a horizontal analytic solution in a rational frame in which the connection is regular at the specified center. This is a condition on a differential equation. The rank, frame, center, and curve carrying each such equation are fixed.

<a id="stmt-int-height"></a>
<div id="int:height" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Conditional height bound). *Fix an ambient dimension $N$, a bound $s_0$ for the number of equations, an integer $b$ bounding the degrees of the polynomial combinations below, and a real constant $C_0\geq2$. Fix also a finite list of algebraic regular germs and ordinary horizontal matrix germs on fixed algebraic curves over number fields. Dual and inverse matrices are allowed in this list. Then there is a constant $C$, depending only on these fixed data, with the following property.*

*Let $d,h\geq2$ and let $z=(a,U)\in\mathbb A^N(\overline{\mathbb Q})$ with $a\ne0$. Suppose a weighted subcollection $V$ and $1\leq s\leq s_0$ equations <a id="int:equations"></a>$$\label{int:equations}
 E_\alpha(a,U)=\sum_{j\geq0}a^j e_{\alpha,j}(U)=0
 \quad(1\leq\alpha\leq s)$$ satisfy the following conditions.*

<a id="int:local-input"></a>
1.  *The point and the selected places satisfy <a id="int:global-input"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
     h_{\mathrm{aff}}(z)&\leq C_0d^{C_0}h,
     &|a|_v&<1\quad(v\in V),\label{int:global-input}\\
     \sum_{v\in V}w_v\log |a|_v^{-1}
       &\geq \frac{h}{C_0d^{C_0}},
     &\sum_{v\in V}w_v\log^+\|U\|_v
       &\leq C_0d^{C_0}(1+\log h)^{C_0}.
     \label{int:local-input}
    \end{aligned}$$ Here $\|U\|_v$ is the maximum of the coordinate absolute values.*

<a id="int:coefficient-input"></a>
2.  *The coefficients are polynomials over a number field containing the target and equation data, with $$\label{int:coefficient-input}
     \deg e_{\alpha,j}\leq C_0(1+j),\qquad
     h_{\mathrm{aff}}(e_{\alpha,j})
      \leq C_0(1+j+d)^{C_0}(1+\log h)^{C_0}.$$ The same equations are used at every selected embedding. They converge there at the target, and there are numbers $b_v\geq0$ such that, for all $\ell\geq0$ and all $\alpha$, <a id="int:tail-input"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
     \left|\sum_{j\geq\ell}a^j e_{\alpha,j}(U)\right|_v
       &\leq |a|_v^\ell\exp((\ell+1)b_v),\label{int:tail-input}\\
     \sum_{v\in V}w_vb_v
       &\leq C_0d^{C_0}(1+\log h)^{C_0}.
    \end{aligned}$$*

3.  *As analytic functions in the variables $(a,U)$, the $E_\alpha$ are polynomial combinations of degree at most $b$ of the coordinates and the fixed germs evaluated at $a$, and possibly at $aR$, where $R$ is one of the coordinates of $U$. Their constant coefficients may depend on the target. The algebraic germs are regular functions on the specified local curve charts. Thus every expression is also a convergent complex analytic germ near any finite point of $a=0$.*

4.  *Let $W\subseteq\mathbb A^N_{\overline{\mathbb Q}}$ be any irreducible closed subvariety containing $z$ on which $a$ is nonconstant. For each prime divisor $D$ in its finite normalization lying above a component of $W\cap\{a=0\}$, at least one $E_\alpha$ is not identically zero as an analytic germ on the local branch at a general point of $D$.*

*Then $h\leq Cd^C$.*

</div>

The quantifier in condition <span class="upright">(iv)</span> includes varieties defined using the coefficients of the equations and the auxiliary polynomials constructed below. It concerns the germ on a whole local branch, rather than its value on the boundary divisor. No bound on the degree of the field of equation coefficients is part of this theorem.

For the elliptic-square application, the inputs to conditions <span class="upright">(i)–(iii)</span> are assembled in [loc:groups](#loc:groups), and condition <span class="upright">(iv)</span> is verified in [nid:nonidentity](#nid:nonidentity); the resulting height bound is stated in [nid:height](#nid:height). We next explain why the theorem requires nonidentity on every algebraic branch, and why small positive local logarithms are needed in addition to a global height bound.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Why the graph gives a height bound

The proof constructs an auxiliary polynomial of degree $L$ whose value at the target gains a factor $a^n$ at the selected places. To make the product formula useful, the construction must allow a sufficiently large ratio $n/L$. The graph construction makes this possible by lowering the dimension of the boundary on which the interpolation conditions are imposed.

Start with $W=\mathbb A^N$. During the proof, $W$ will be an irreducible algebraic subvariety containing $z$, with controlled degree and defining heights. Write $k=\dim W$ and first suppose that $a$ varies on $W$ and that $W\cap\{a=0\}$ is nonempty. Condition <span class="upright">(iv)</span> and an ordinary differential multiplicity estimate give an integral linear combination $E=\sum c_\alpha E_\alpha=\sum_{j\geq0}a^je_j(U)$ and an integer $m$ such that its truncation $$F=E_{<m}:=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}a^je_j(U)
 \quad\text{satisfies}\quad
 \mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_D F<m\mathop{\mathrm{ord}}_D a$$ on every normalized boundary divisor $D$. The coefficients $c_\alpha$ and $m$ are polynomially bounded in $\deg W$. This strict inequality means that $F/a^m$ has a pole at the generic point of every such divisor; a nonzero restriction of $E$ to the boundary itself is not required.

Let $W'$ be the affine closure of the graph of $q=F/a^m$ over $a\ne0$. It has dimension $k$. Its boundary over $a=0$ has no point above a general point of any boundary divisor of $W$, because $q$ has a pole there. Thus the projection of $W'\cap\{a=0\}$ to $W$ has dimension at most $k-2$. Imposing $q=-e_m(U)$ fixes the graph coordinate, so $$\dim\bigl(W'\cap\{a=0,\ q+e_m(U)=0\}\bigr)\leq k-2.$$ For $k=1$ this intersection is empty. This is a bound on the indicated incidence, not on the whole graph boundary, whose fibers may have positive dimension.

The lifted target $z'=(a,U,F(z)/a^m)$ has a different useful property. At each selected place $E(z)=0$, and therefore $$q(z')=-a^{-m}\sum_{j\geq m}a^je_j(U).$$ The tail estimate bounds the positive local logarithm of this new coordinate by $(m+1)b_v$, with the errors enlarged to accommodate the chosen integral combination. Hence $z'$ still has small positive local logarithms, although its global height can be proportional to $h$.

Choose equations generating an ideal $I$ whose zero set is $W'$ and put $$F_n=q+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a^je_{m+j}(U).$$ The dimension bound above permits a polynomial $P$, nonzero on $W'$, with $$\deg P\leq L,\qquad P\in(I,a^n,F_n),\qquad n=cL,$$ for any prescribed positive integer $c$, with $L$ polynomially bounded in $c$ and the degree bounds of the construction. Here membership is in the displayed ideal itself, even if $I$ is nonradical. The reason for the freedom in $c$ is a count of linear conditions. For $k\geq2$, reducing successively through $n$ powers of $a$ imposes $O(n(L+n)^{k-2})$ conditions, with a constant polynomial in the defining degree bounds. Restrictions of degree-$\leq L$ polynomials to $W'$ span a space of dimension at least $\binom{L+k}{k}$. For $n=cL$ these grow respectively as $L^{k-1}$ and $L^k$. When $k=1$ there are no conditions. This saving of one power of $L$ is the purpose of the graph.

At the selected places, evaluating the ideal-membership certificate and using the tail bound gives the factor $|a|_v^n$. Even if the certificate has degree larger than $L$, its degree multiplies only the small positive local logarithms of $z'$. At the remaining places one evaluates $P$ itself, so the global height cost is proportional to $Lh$. Consequently, if $P(z')\ne0$, the product formula gives an inequality of the form $$0\leq\left(-\frac n{A(d)}+L C(d)\right)h
        +D(d)(1+\log h)^B,$$ where $A,C,D$ are fixed polynomials and $B$ is fixed. The polynomials $A,C$ are determined before $c$ is chosen. Taking $c$ sufficiently large, still polynomially bounded in $d$, makes the coefficient of $h$ negative and yields a polynomial bound for $h$ in $d$.

If that bound does not hold, $P(z')=0$. Substituting $q=F/a^m$ and clearing denominators gives a polynomial nonzero on $W$ and zero at $z$. An irreducible component through $z$ of its zero set in $W$ has smaller dimension and controlled degree and height. This explains the universal quantifier in condition <span class="upright">(iv)</span>: the next variety is chosen using the equations and the auxiliary polynomial. After at most $N$ such steps, either the height is bounded or one reaches a variety disjoint from $a=0$. If $G_1,\ldots,G_r$ define that variety, a polynomial identity $1=aA+\sum_\rho G_\rho A_\rho$ and the small positive local logarithms give the bound directly. Appendix <a href="#int:proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="int:proof">9</a> proves these degree, height, and multiplicity assertions and the descent in detail.

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### The geometric systems and a finite list of correspondences

We first fix the curves, the family, and an elliptic-square center. The trace-free self-adjoint endomorphisms form a five-dimensional orthogonal system. Its monodromy gives an alternative for two varying points: the two systems have product monodromy, or their joint image lies in a polarized isogeny correspondence. The final result of this section reduces the latter alternative to a fixed finite list. This list is chosen before the auxiliary levels of [inc:markings](#inc:markings).

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Fixed covers and the center

<a id="stmt-geo-setup"></a>
<div id="geo:setup" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5**. *In proving [intro:squares](#intro:squares), we may work with a smooth geometrically irreducible curve $S$ over a fixed number field, a principally polarized abelian scheme $\mathscr A\to S$, and a nonconstant finite map from $S$ onto a dense open of the original curve $C$. We may equip the family with full symplectic levels at two distinct fixed primes at least three. Under the supposition that the required elliptic-square locus is infinite, we may fix a point $t_0\in S(\overline{\mathbb Q})$ in that locus.*

<a id="geo:degree-descent"></a>
*Further fixed finite covers, normalization, and removal of finite sets preserve this reduction. After enlarging a fixed field of definition $K$, there is a constant $b$ such that every selected lift $t$ of a retained coarse point $s$ satisfies $$\label{geo:degree-descent}
 [K(s):K]\leq [K(t):K]\leq b[K(s):K].$$ The smooth projective completion of $S$ and the divisor $[t_0]$ are available even when $C$ is complete.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-setup"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-setup</summary>

*Proof.* Choose distinct primes $r_1,r_2\geq3$ and put $N=r_1r_2$. These preliminary levels give a fine moduli cover. The auxiliary incidence primes will be chosen separately in [inc:markings](#inc:markings), after the correspondence list has been fixed. After adjoining the requisite roots of unity, the fine Siegel moduli space $\mathcal A_2(N)$ carries a universal principally polarized abelian scheme and has a finite surjective forgetful map to the coarse space $\mathcal A_2$; its analytic uniformization and the algebraicity of the universal family are described in (Milne 2013, Theorem 4.6 and Corollary 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MilneModuli2013](#ref-MilneModuli2013)]. Take an irreducible component of its pullback to $C$ that dominates $C$, and normalize. Normalization is finite for a curve over a field of characteristic zero. The resulting curve is smooth, and pullback supplies the abelian scheme. Removing the preimages of any finite exceptional set of coarse points leaves a finite surjective map onto the complementary open of $C$.

All these varieties, components, morphisms, and level structures are defined over a number field after a fixed enlargement. The fibers of a fixed finite map have bounded length; on a dense open this length is its degree. The residue field degree of any point in such a fiber is at most that length. This proves <a href="#geo:degree-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:degree-descent">[geo:degree-descent]</a>, with the degrees of any additional fixed covers included in $b$. It also shows that the image of every omitted finite set is finite. An infinite coarse locus therefore has infinitely many lifts after every such fixed omission, and we choose one of them as $t_0$. Later covers may be supplied with a fixed lift of this point; we then reuse $S,t_0$ for the refined data. Open charts and frames used at the center are chosen to contain it.

A smooth curve over a number field has a smooth projective completion. The point $t_0$ is an interior point of the moduli family, and its divisor has positive degree on that completion. This construction uses no geometric boundary point and imposes no condition on the arithmetic reduction of $\mathscr A_{t_0}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="geo:coarse-curves"></a>
Fix a number field $K_C$ defining the original coarse curve. There are only finitely many coarse conjugate curves $$\label{geo:coarse-curves}
 C_1,\ldots,C_e\subset\mathcal A_2,$$ obtained by applying embeddings of $K_C$ into $\overline{\mathbb Q}$. All conjugate families used below have coarse images in this list. Enlarging the field to define centers, levels, or individual endomorphisms does not add coarse images: the image of $C$ depends only on the restriction of the embedding to $K_C$. We always retain the closed coarse curves in <a href="#geo:coarse-curves" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:coarse-curves">[geo:coarse-curves]</a>, even when their working parameter curves have been restricted to open subsets.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The five-dimensional orthogonal system

<a id="geo:operator-space"></a>
Let $H=R_1\mathscr A_*\mathbb Q$ denote rational covariant first homology. The polarization gives an alternating form $\psi$, with its usual Tate twist in the different realizations. For an operator $D$, write $D^\dagger$ for the adjoint defined by $\psi(Dv,w)=\psi(v,D^\dagger w)$. Define $$\label{geo:operator-space}
 \mathcal P=\{D\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(H):D^\dagger=D,\ \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_H(D)=0\},
 \qquad \langle D,E\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_H(DE).$$ These definitions commute with all the realization functors used later. On actual endomorphisms, $\dagger$ is the Rosati involution of the given principal polarization.

<a id="stmt-geo-five-space"></a>
<div id="geo:five-space" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:integral-lattice"></a>
**Proposition 6**. *The system $\mathcal P$ has rank five and a split nondegenerate trace pairing. Conjugation induces a surjective morphism $$\mathop{\mathrm{GSp}}_4\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathcal P)$$ with scalar kernel. Its restriction to $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$ is the spin covering, with kernel $\{1,-1\}$, and $\mathcal P$ is absolutely irreducible. For every odd prime $\ell$, the lattice $$\label{geo:integral-lattice}
 \Lambda_\ell=
 \{D\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathbb Z_\ell}(T_\ell\mathscr A_t):
       D^\dagger=D,\ \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D)=0\}$$ is free of rank five with unimodular trace pairing.*

*If $\mathscr A_t$ is isogenous to the square of a non-CM elliptic curve, then <a id="geo:hodge-plane"></a>$$\label{geo:hodge-plane}
 F_t=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}^0(\mathscr A_t)\cap\mathcal P_t$$ is a rational two-dimensional plane on which the trace pairing is positive definite. Here the intersection denotes Hodge endomorphisms in the Betti realization, equivalently actual rational endomorphisms in every realization.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-five-space"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-five-space</summary>

<a id="geo:matrix-model"></a>
*Proof.* Use a symplectic basis, with matrix $J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_2\\-I_2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. The equations $D^{\mathsf t}J=JD$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}D=0$ give exactly $$\label{geo:matrix-model}
 D=\begin{pmatrix}A&uK_2\\vK_2&A^{\mathsf t}\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&-a\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad K_2=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}.$$ Multiplication gives <a id="geo:trace-form"></a>$$\label{geo:trace-form}
 D^2=(a^2+bc-uv)I_4,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D^2)=4(a^2+bc-uv).$$ Thus the form is the orthogonal sum of a nonzero one-dimensional form and two hyperbolic planes. Its Gram determinant in the five coordinates displayed above is $64$, a unit over every $\mathbb Z_\ell$ with $\ell$ odd. A principal polarization makes the Tate module symplectically unimodular, so the same calculation proves <a href="#geo:integral-lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:integral-lattice">[geo:integral-lattice]</a>.

Conjugation by a symplectic similitude preserves both conditions in <a href="#geo:operator-space" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:operator-space">[geo:operator-space]</a> and the trace pairing. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{GSp}}_4$ is connected, its orthogonal image has determinant one. The induced action of $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$ is nontrivial, as is already seen by conjugating the matrices in <a href="#geo:matrix-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:matrix-model">[geo:matrix-model]</a>. The simple Lie algebra $\mathfrak{sp}_4$ consequently has zero kernel in this action. Both $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$ have dimension ten, so the image is all of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$. The finite kernel is central in the connected group $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$ and is exactly $\{1,-1\}$. Over an algebraic closure a similitude is a scalar times a symplectic element, which proves the scalar-kernel assertion for $\mathop{\mathrm{GSp}}_4$ and identifies the restriction as the spin covering. The natural representation of $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$ is irreducible over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero: the orbit of any nonzero vector, whether isotropic or not, spans the entire quadratic space. This proves absolute irreducibility. Equivalently, $D%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto\EndAccSupp{}\psi(D\cdot,\cdot)$ identifies the self-adjoint space with alternating two-forms, and the trace-free summand with the primitive exterior-square representation.

For the last assertion, put $A=\mathscr A_t$. The equivalence between complex abelian varieties up to isogeny and polarizable rational Hodge structures of type $(-1,0),(0,-1)$ identifies $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}^0(A)$ with the Hodge endomorphisms of $H_1(A,\mathbb Q)$ (Milne 1995, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[MilneShimura1995](#ref-MilneShimura1995)]. Write $H_1(A,\mathbb Q)=H_1(E,\mathbb Q)\otimes\mathbb Q^2$ using the given unpolarized isogeny. Since $E$ has no CM, its endomorphism algebra is $\mathbb Q$; the Hodge endomorphism algebra of $A$ is therefore $\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_2(\mathbb Q)$. An alternating polarization on this tensor product has the form $\psi_E\otimes B$, where $B$ is a symmetric rational form on $\mathbb Q^2$. Indeed, each of its four matrix entries is a Hodge alternating pairing on $H_1(E,\mathbb Q)$, hence a scalar multiple of $\psi_E$; alternation of the total form makes these four scalars symmetric. Positivity of the polarization, after choosing the sign of $\psi_E$, makes $B$ positive definite. The Rosati involution on $\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}_2(\mathbb Q)$ is consequently the adjoint for $B$.

Its self-adjoint part has dimension three and contains the identity. The homology trace on this algebra is twice the ordinary matrix trace, so imposing trace zero leaves dimension two. Over $\mathbb R$, conjugation by the positive square root of $B$ turns a self-adjoint matrix $D$ into a symmetric matrix $D_0$. Hence, for $D\ne0$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{H_1(A)}(D^2)=2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D_0^2)>0.$$ This proves the asserted positivity for every principal polarization, without requiring the initial isogeny from $E^2$ to preserve it. ◻

</details>

Fix a labeled pair $\mathbf B=(B_1,B_2)$ of linearly independent integral endomorphisms spanning $F_0:=F_{t_0}$; clear denominators in a rational basis to obtain it. At any target and prime, the plane lattice used for reduction is <a id="geo:saturated-plane"></a>$$\label{geo:saturated-plane}
 \Pi_{t,\ell}=(F_t\otimes\mathbb Q_\ell)\cap\Lambda_\ell.$$ It is saturated and has rank two, so its image in $\Lambda_\ell/\ell\Lambda_\ell$ has dimension two. We do not identify this image with the span of a chosen pair reduced modulo $\ell$: that pair can fail to be a lattice basis. At the fixed center, the restriction of the pairing to $\Pi_{t_0,\ell}$ is nondegenerate modulo $\ell$ outside finitely many primes. To see finiteness, intersect $F_0$ with the integral Betti operator lattice, choose a $\mathbb Z$-basis of this intersection, and exclude the prime divisors of its nonzero Gram determinant. Betti–Tate comparison gives precisely the lattices above.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Full and joint monodromy

<a id="stmt-geo-monodromy"></a>
<div id="geo:monodromy" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7**. *The connected algebraic monodromy of $H$ is $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$, and that of $\mathcal P$ is $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$. This remains true on every fixed finite cover, on a dense open, and for the conjugate families under consideration. The same connected monodromy is obtained by pullback to a smooth connected algebraic variety mapping dominantly to the base curve.*

*Suppose a smooth connected algebraic variety $W$ maps dominantly to two such curves. The connected joint monodromy on the two five-dimensional systems is either $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5\times\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$, or the coarse joint image of $W$ is contained in a polarized isogeny correspondence. If only the first projection varies, its connected monodromy is still $\mathop{\mathrm{SO}}_5$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-monodromy"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-monodromy</summary>

*Proof.* Hodge genericity in Siegel moduli means that the generic Mumford–Tate group of $H$ is $\mathop{\mathrm{GSp}}_4$. The variation is pure and polarizable and comes from an abelian scheme over a smooth algebraic curve. Thus it is among the good variations to which the connected-monodromy normality theorem applies: connected algebraic monodromy is a normal subgroup of the derived generic Mumford–Tate group (André 1992, sec. 5, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Andre1992](#ref-Andre1992)]. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$ is almost simple, the connected monodromy is trivial or all of $\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}_4$.

Here is a direct exclusion of the trivial possibility. It would make the monodromy group finite, and a finite cover would trivialize the integral local system. The period map would then be a single-valued holomorphic map from a smooth algebraic curve to Siegel space. Realize Siegel space as a bounded domain in $\mathbb C^3$. Each coordinate of this map is bounded near each of the finitely many missing points of the smooth projective completion, so has a removable singularity there. Every extended coordinate is constant on the projective curve. The family would have constant moduli image, contrary to its domination of $C$. This also explains concretely the applicability of (André 1992, sec. 6, Remark 1) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Andre1992](#ref-Andre1992)]. The monodromy conclusion is independent of the Mumford–Tate group at the chosen center. [geo:five-space](#geo:five-space) now gives the assertion on $\mathcal P$.

A nonconstant morphism between smooth algebraic curves restricts, after removal of finitely many points, to a finite etale morphism onto a dense open. Its topological fundamental group therefore has image of finite index in that of this open; the latter maps surjectively to the fundamental group of the original curve. Finite-index subgroups have the same connected Zariski closure. This proves invariance under finite covers and open restrictions. For a dominant map from a higher-dimensional smooth variety, choose an algebraic curve in that variety whose projection is nonconstant, and normalize it. Such a curve is obtained by successive general affine hyperplane sections through a point where the differential of the projection is nonzero.
